`milestone_visual.tex` contiene la plantilla compartida. La copia para construcción web está en `web/templates/milestone_visual.tex`; ambas deben coincidir. El contenido se mantiene separado del formato.

Antes de publicar o entregar: compilar, comprobar longitud solicitada, renderizar todas las páginas, revisar desbordamientos/tablas/enlaces y contrastar con el Milestone 1. Cambiar únicamente datos propios de la etapa, contenido, estado y versiones; conservar el lenguaje visual.

Milestone 1 y el borrador v0.1 no se modifican. La versión v0.2 de Milestone 2 corrige la presentación y permanece como borrador no enviado.

profiles=[

| **Centrality-aware ML · externo** · [pdf-30](https://networking.ifip.org/2025/images/Net25_papers/1571131460.pdf) | other | No se demostró presión futura | **Aprendizaje con demanda, hora y centralidad de red.** Relaciona características operativas y topológicas con presión en otra red. Relaciones de demanda, hora y centralidad a presión; no lead de presión verificado. |
| **GraphConvWat · soporte** · [repo-49](https://github.com/BME-SmartLab/GraphConvWat/tree/be97b45fbc7dfdba22bb1ee406424a7c568120e5) | other | No se demostró presión futura | **Reconstrucción espacial con convoluciones en grafos.** Repositorio de soporte del linaje ChebNet; no hereda por sí solo su shift temporal. Repositorio de reconstrucción espacial; su uso como base de ChebNet no transfiere automáticamente un predictor temporal. |
| **GASTL · consulta editorial** · [editorial-gastl](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.watres.2025.124475) | ltown | No se demostró presión futura | **Estimación espacial con transferencia; consulta parcial.** Describe transferencia de estimación entre condiciones/redes; lead sin validar. Transferencia de estimación espacial; protocolo/horizonte de presión futura no validado en la consulta parcial. |

P5 se evalúa explícitamente como target de presión futura, con cuatro pasos a 30 minutos. |

Secuencias futuras y GRU/atención descritos; la equivalencia física presión/head/demanda de la salida h no queda resuelta en el texto parcial. |

## Subconjunto: presión futura confirmada

Tres casos sobre L-Town o modificaciones y un antecedente externo. El objetivo final se mantiene visible; no se filtra solo por el título del artículo.

### ChebNet · historia como canales de una GNN

**Objetivo final:** Detectar y localizar fugas. **Ámbito:** L-Town; nominal para train, SCADA para diagnóstico.

**Salida / nodo:** Presiones de nodos del grafo; incluye nodos monitorizados y no monitorizados

**Historia:** T=3 seleccionado; alternativas 1,2,3,6,12,24 registros

**Target y horizonte:** p(u+5 min), donde u es la última entrada

**Técnica:** Cuatro convoluciones espectrales Chebyshev; K=[240,120,20], filtros=[120,60,30], SiLU, salida sigmoide; Adam/weight decay.

**Mecanismo temporal:** La historia se apila como canales. El loader corta hasta i−1 y usa y[i]. No hace falta una LSTM para que exista predicción temporal.

**Preparación:** Presiones parciales/mascara, grafo nominal y escalado; la escala anterior al split requiere revisión causal.

**Actualización:** Modelo entrenado; no actualización online de etiquetas evaluada como nuestro +15.

**Evaluación:** Resultados reportados de fugas: 19 TP, 138 FP, 4 FN; no MAE +15/persistencia.

**Evidencia:** P10, PDF pp.3–5; loader conserva alineación exacta

**Código:** data_loader.py:285–290; predict.py; commit 864a99041af6

**Límites:** T=3 es historia; no horizonte 15 min. La presión nominal saludable y la presión observada durante fuga son targets distintos.

**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** ChebNet predictor (comparado con un reconstructor actual).

### SAM-kNN · regresión adaptativa desde historia resumida

**Objetivo final:** Diagnosticar fugas y fallas. **Ámbito:** L-Town A modificada; diez escenarios propios.

**Salida / nodo:** Un sensor objetivo por virtual sensor; 29 sensores de presión en el experimento

**Historia:** Promedio de las tres filas previas, con time_win_len=4

**Target y horizonte:** La cuarta fila: p(u+5 min) tras la última entrada

**Técnica:** kNN con memorias STM, LTM y combinada; selección por error y limpieza/compresión de muestras.

**Mecanismo temporal:** No aprende una secuencia recurrente: resume tres registros en un vector y regresa la siguiente presión. El input del sensor objetivo se excluye al formar su virtual sensor.

**Preparación:** Primeros 3.000 ejemplos como entrenamiento según script; flujo procesado en lotes de 200.

**Actualización:** Predicción/detección de lote antes de fit. Para +15, esperar la llegada de y del origen u en u+15.

**Evaluación:** TP/FN por evento y FP por alarma; detecta cinco fugas con FP 48,20,3,20,17.

**Evidencia:** P26, PDF pp.8–10 + preprocesamiento conservado

**Código:** LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py:19,24,27; experiments.py:17,21,157,162; models.py; commit 9116ec99bf9f

**Límites:** Lotes y detección no prueban una latencia de alarma de cinco minutos. Los escenarios no se distribuyen y no son SCADA original.

**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** SAM-kNN adaptativo; kNN online (River; k=5, ventana=500, media ponderada); Regresión lineal online (River; baseline).

### AIGNN · GNN con transferencia de razonamiento algorítmico

**Objetivo final:** Detectar y localizar fugas. **Ámbito:** L-Town; simulación nominal para entrenamiento.

**Salida / nodo:** Presión nodal del grafo; mínimo un nodo queda incluido

**Historia:** x(t−T)…x(t−1); T es historia, no lead

**Target y horizonte:** y(t) respecto a última entrada t−1: un paso físico

**Técnica:** Processor preentrenado en Ford–Fulkerson (1.000 grafos de 16 nodos); encoder/decoder de presión y variantes ChebNetIN/ChebNetEMB.

**Mecanismo temporal:** Historial de presión al encoder; las características max-flow enriquecen el predictor. Las iteraciones de razonamiento en el grafo no se confunden con minutos de forecast.

**Preparación:** Modelo nominal proporciona labels; 2018 ajusta umbrales de diagnóstico; 2019 evalúa detección; pruebas de reubicación de sensores.

**Actualización:** No se afirma protocolo adaptativo online propio.

**Evaluación:** Preprint Table 3: error relativo de predicción; no MAE +15 ni skill de persistencia.

**Evidencia:** P22, PDF pp.15–19; inputs históricos y outputs separados

**Código:** No snapshot de código de autor AIGNN conservado; evidencia de formulación en el preprint.

**Límites:** Las métricas del preprint no se fusionan con la versión publicada 2026.

**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** ChebNet baseline; AIGNN; ChebNetIN; ChebNetEMB.

### hDA/hDS-RNN · encoder–decoder LSTM con atención

**Objetivo final:** Pronosticar estados futuros. **Ámbito:** Otra red real: ciudad denominada CZ por los autores; NO L-Town.

**Salida / nodo:** Sensor de presión P5 (también evalúa caudal F8)

**Historia:** 60 registros a 30 min; histórico del target incluido

**Target y horizonte:** P5 en los cuatro instantes posteriores

**Técnica:** Encoder y decoder LSTM; atención espacial híbrida (entre series y a través de historia) + atención temporal del decoder.

**Mecanismo temporal:** Seq2seq produce la secuencia y(T+1)…y(T+4), no reconstruye el input.

**Preparación:** Diferenciación de primer orden, descomposición estacional aprendida en train, reconstrucción de serie; split cronológico ≈4:1:1; MSE/Adam.

**Actualización:** Entrenamiento por lotes; dropout y validación de hiperparámetros.

**Evaluación:** Table 1 incluye P5 como target. La extensión a diez pasos mostrada en Fig.4 es de caudal; no se asigna automáticamente a presión.

**Evidencia:** P23, PDF pp.2–5, 6; targets F8 y P5 explícitos

**Código:** No repositorio de autor verificado/conservado para este caso.

**Límites:** Antecedente metodológico externo: no prueba una solución previa sobre BattLeDIM.

**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** hDA/hDS-RNN; SARIMA; SVR; GBRT; MLP/ANN; Seq2Seq; Transformer; DA/DS-RNN; DA/DS-RNN-II; DSTP-RNN.

## Evidencia incompleta: no descartar ni sumar como confirmado

### VAR multivariado de presión · temporal descrito

**Técnica:** Vector autoregression sobre presión histórica; también menciona series exógenas.

**Documentado:** Estima el patrón normal de presión para residuos/SPC.

**Pendiente:** Orden/ventana exacta, lead y disponibilidad temporal de exógenas no definidos; no código alineado conservado.

**Evidencia:** P08a, PDF pp.2–3. Incluido como temporal descrito, separado de confirmados.

### AGN · estado futuro, target por aclarar

**Técnica:** GGNN/GRU con atención self/external y rasgos nodales.

**Documentado:** El texto editorial describe presión histórica, secuencias futuras y entrenamiento cronológico en L-Town modificada.

**Pendiente:** La salida h y la equivalencia presión/head/demanda no se resuelven con el texto parcial; falta PDF/código validado.

**Evidencia:** Consulta editorial parcial ampliada; no se acredita presión futura como confirmado.

## Casos que no acreditan presión futura

- **D-UKF-AW-GSI:** La medición zh es constante durante el run; k es iteración de estimación, no tiempo futuro. P18 pp.4 y 7.
- **LILA / Leakbusters:** Presión/caudal de la condición actual → referencia actual; no shift de target.
- **GATRes:** Mask de x y comparación contra y del mismo snapshot; no desplazamiento temporal.
- **Artesia · ExtraTrees:** Agregados de todo el día y cross-validation por días; no origen de pronóstico prospectivo documentado.
- **DHI China:** LSTM se utiliza para clasificar áreas con fugas, no para etiquetar presión futura.

## Gaps y objetivo candidato

La predicción temporal de presión ya participa en soluciones de fugas sobre L-Town: no basta proponer “predecir presión”. Los casos confirmados usan un paso de cinco minutos; varios aprenden presión nominal saludable o escenarios modificados. Falta establecer, para nuestra comparación, el desempeño a +15 minutos sobre presión observada bajo un protocolo causal compartido, y el efecto de seleccionar sensores por relación hidráulica y antigüedad. Esa combinación es un aporte candidato, no una afirmación de novedad mundial. Se deben cerrar Tsinghua y AGN, comparar persistencia, regresión con rezagos y un antecedente adaptado a tres pasos, y medir MAE/RMSE por nodo, régimen y retraso. Un horizonte diferente por sí solo no prueba novedad.

## Trazabilidad

CSV: `respaldo/estado_del_arte/clasificacion/sources_classified.csv`. JSON: `classification.json`. Contienen cada archivo/fuente, objetivo, ámbito, estado temporal, razón de clasificación y enlaces. Los hashes y commits están en el JSON. No se ejecutó código de terceros ni se reprodujeron entrenamientos.

def crossref(doi):
    url = 'https://api.crossref.org/works/' + quote(doi, safe='')
    try:
        data, _, _ = get(url)
        obj = json.loads(data)['message']
        name = re.sub(r'[^A-Za-z0-9._-]', '_',doi)
        (ROOT/f'respaldo/literatura/crossref_{name}.json').write_bytes(data)
        return obj
    except Exception as ex:
        record({'url':url,'status':'unavailable','reason':str(ex)})
        return {'DOI':doi,'error':str(ex)}

class TextParser(HTMLParser):
    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__(); self.parts=[]; self.skip=0; self.links=[]
    def handle_starttag(self,tag,attrs):
        if tag in ('script','style'): self.skip+=1
        if tag=='a':
            href=dict(attrs).get('href')
            if href: self.links.append(href)
        if tag in ('p','div','br','tr','h1','h2','h3','li'): self.parts.append('\n')
    def handle_endtag(self,tag):
        if tag in ('script','style'): self.skip=max(0,self.skip-1)
    def handle_data(self,data):
        if not self.skip: self.parts.append(data)

def archive(name,url):
    r=save(url,f'respaldo/fuentes_oficiales/{name}.html')
    path=ROOT/f'respaldo/fuentes_oficiales/{name}.html'
    if r['status']=='downloaded':
        parser=TextParser(); parser.feed(path.read_text(encoding='utf-8',errors='replace'))
        text=re.sub(r'\n\s*\n+', '\n\n',''.join(parser.parts))
        path.with_suffix('.txt').write_text(text,encoding='utf-8')
        path.with_suffix('.links.json').write_text(json.dumps(parser.links,indent=2),encoding='utf-8')
    return r

if __name__=='__main__':
    import sys
    mode=sys.argv[1]
    if mode=='papers':
        with cf.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as pool:
            results=list(pool.map(lambda x:save(x[1],f'respaldo/literatura/pdf/{x[0]}.pdf'),PDFS))
        (ROOT/'respaldo/auditoria/pdf_catalog.json').write_text(json.dumps(results,indent=2),encoding='utf-8')
        print(json.dumps([{k:v for k,v in x.items() if k in ('path','status','reason','bytes')} for x in results],indent=2))
        with cf.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4) as pool: refs=list(pool.map(crossref,DOIS))
        (ROOT/'respaldo/literatura/crossref_catalog.json').write_text(json.dumps(refs,indent=2,ensure_ascii=False),encoding='utf-8')
    if mode=='pages':
        tasks=[('official_papers','https://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/?page_id=236'),
               ('official_results','https://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/?page_id=270'),
               ('official_dataset','https://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/?page_id=24'),
               ('IWA_encoder_decoder_2024','https://iwaponline.com/ws/article/24/8/2727/103964/The-performance-of-encoder-decoder-neural-networks'),
               ('kaggle_mirror','https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim')]
        with cf.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=3) as pool: print(json.dumps(list(pool.map(lambda x:archive(*x),tasks)),indent=2))
    if mode=='notebooks':
        count=0
        for folder in (ROOT/'respaldo/repositorios').iterdir():
            if not (folder/'tree.json').exists(): continue
            provenance=json.loads((folder/'PROVENANCE.json').read_text())
            repo=provenance['repository']; sha=provenance['sha']
            for item in json.loads((folder/'tree.json').read_text())['tree']:
                name=item['path']
                if name.endswith('.ipynb') and 3_000_000 <= item.get('size',0) < 80_000_000:
                    result=save(f'https://raw.githubusercontent.com/{repo}/{sha}/{quote(name)}',
                                str((folder/'snapshot'/name).relative_to(ROOT)).replace('\\','/'))
                    if result['status']=='downloaded': count+=1
            for nb in (folder/'snapshot').rglob('*.ipynb'):
                obj=json.loads(nb.read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
                blocks=[]
                for idx, cell in enumerate(obj.get('cells',[])):
                    source=''.join(cell.get('source',[]))
                    blocks.append(f'\n# CELL {idx} ({cell.get("cell_type")})\n{source}\n')
                target=folder/'lectura_notebooks'/nb.relative_to(folder/'snapshot')
                target=target.with_suffix('.txt'); target.parent.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)
                target.write_text(''.join(blocks),encoding='utf-8')
        print('Additional notebooks acquired:',count)

Taxonomía de métodos; referencias a forecasting no son un experimento propio. |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Subconjunto: presión futura confirmada\\r\\n\\r\\nTres casos sobre L-Town o modificaciones y un antecedente externo. El objetivo final se mantiene visible; no se filtra solo por el título del artículo.\\r\\n\\r\\n### ChebNet · historia como canales de una GNN\\r\\n\\r\\n**Objetivo final:** Detectar y localizar fugas. **Ámbito:** L-Town; nominal para train, SCADA para diagnóstico.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Salida / nodo:** Presiones de nodos del grafo; incluye nodos monitorizados y no monitorizados\\r\\n\\r\\n**Historia:** T=3 seleccionado; alternativas 1,2,3,6,12,24 registros\\r\\n\\r\\n**Target y horizonte:** p(u+5 min), donde u es la última entrada\\r\\n\\r\\n**Técnica:** Cuatro convoluciones espectrales Chebyshev; K=[240,120,20], filtros=[120,60,30], SiLU, salida sigmoide; Adam/weight decay.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Mecanismo temporal:** La historia se apila como canales. El loader corta hasta i−1 y usa y[i]. No hace falta una LSTM para que exista predicción temporal.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Preparación:** Presiones parciales/mascara, grafo nominal y escalado; la escala anterior al split requiere revisión causal.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Actualización:** Modelo entrenado; no actualización online de etiquetas evaluada como nuestro +15.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluación:** Resultados reportados de fugas: 19 TP, 138 FP, 4 FN; no MAE +15/persistencia.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidencia:** P10, PDF pp.3–5; loader conserva alineación exacta\\r\\n\\r\\n**Código:** data_loader.py:285–290; predict.py; commit 864a99041af6\\r\\n\\r\\n**Límites:** T=3 es historia; no horizonte 15 min. La presión nominal saludable y la presión observada durante fuga son targets distintos.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** ChebNet predictor (comparado con un reconstructor actual).\\r\\n\\r\\n### SAM-kNN · regresión adaptativa desde historia resumida\\r\\n\\r\\n**Objetivo final:** Diagnosticar fugas y fallas. **Ámbito:** L-Town A modificada; diez escenarios propios.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Salida / nodo:** Un sensor objetivo por virtual sensor; 29 sensores de presión en el experimento\\r\\n\\r\\n**Historia:** Promedio de las tres filas previas, con time_win_len=4\\r\\n\\r\\n**Target y horizonte:** La cuarta fila: p(u+5 min) tras la última entrada\\r\\n\\r\\n**Técnica:** kNN con memorias STM, LTM y combinada; selección por error y limpieza/compresión de muestras.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Mecanismo temporal:** No aprende una secuencia recurrente: resume tres registros en un vector y regresa la siguiente presión. El input del sensor objetivo se excluye al formar su virtual sensor.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Preparación:** Primeros 3.000 ejemplos como entrenamiento según script; flujo procesado en lotes de 200.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Actualización:** Predicción/detección de lote antes de fit. Para +15, esperar la llegada de y del origen u en u+15.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluación:** TP/FN por evento y FP por alarma; detecta cinco fugas con FP 48,20,3,20,17.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidencia:** P26, PDF pp.8–10 + preprocesamiento conservado\\r\\n\\r\\n**Código:** LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py:19,24,27; experiments.py:17,21,157,162; models.py; commit 9116ec99bf9f\\r\\n\\r\\n**Límites:** Lotes y detección no prueban una latencia de alarma de cinco minutos. Los escenarios no se distribuyen y no son SCADA original.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** SAM-kNN adaptativo; kNN online (River; k=5, ventana=500, media ponderada); Regresión lineal online (River; baseline).\\r\\n\\r\\n### AIGNN · GNN con transferencia de razonamiento algorítmico\\r\\n\\r\\n**Objetivo final:** Detectar y localizar fugas. **Ámbito:** L-Town; simulación nominal para entrenamiento.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Salida / nodo:** Presión nodal del grafo; mínimo un nodo queda incluido\\r\\n\\r\\n**Historia:** x(t−T)…x(t−1); T es historia, no lead\\r\\n\\r\\n**Target y horizonte:** y(t) respecto a última entrada t−1: un paso físico\\r\\n\\r\\n**Técnica:** Processor preentrenado en Ford–Fulkerson (1.000 grafos de 16 nodos); encoder/decoder de presión y variantes ChebNetIN/ChebNetEMB.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Mecanismo temporal:** Historial de presión al encoder; las características max-flow enriquecen el predictor. Las iteraciones de razonamiento en el grafo no se confunden con minutos de forecast.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Preparación:** Modelo nominal proporciona labels; 2018 ajusta umbrales de diagnóstico; 2019 evalúa detección; pruebas de reubicación de sensores.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Actualización:** No se afirma protocolo adaptativo online propio.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluación:** Preprint Table 3: error relativo de predicción; no MAE +15 ni skill de persistencia.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidencia:** P22, PDF pp.15–19; inputs históricos y outputs separados\\r\\n\\r\\n**Código:** No snapshot de código de autor AIGNN conservado; evidencia de formulación en el preprint.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Límites:** Las métricas del preprint no se fusionan con la versión publicada 2026.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** ChebNet baseline; AIGNN; ChebNetIN; ChebNetEMB.\\r\\n\\r\\n### hDA/hDS-RNN · encoder–decoder LSTM con atención\\r\\n\\r\\n**Objetivo final:** Pronosticar estados futuros. **Ámbito:** Otra red real: ciudad denominada CZ por los autores; NO L-Town.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Salida / nodo:** Sensor de presión P5 (también evalúa caudal F8)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Historia:** 60 registros a 30 min; histórico del target incluido\\r\\n\\r\\n**Target y horizonte:** P5 en los cuatro instantes posteriores\\r\\n\\r\\n**Técnica:** Encoder y decoder LSTM; atención espacial híbrida (entre series y a través de historia) + atención temporal del decoder.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Mecanismo temporal:** Seq2seq produce la secuencia y(T+1)…y(T+4), no reconstruye el input.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Preparación:** Diferenciación de primer orden, descomposición estacional aprendida en train, reconstrucción de serie; split cronológico ≈4:1:1; MSE/Adam.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Actualización:** Entrenamiento por lotes; dropout y validación de hiperparámetros.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evaluación:** Table 1 incluye P5 como target. La extensión a diez pasos mostrada en Fig.4 es de caudal; no se asigna automáticamente a presión.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidencia:** P23, PDF pp.2–5, 6; targets F8 y P5 explícitos\\r\\n\\r\\n**Código:** No repositorio de autor verificado/conservado para este caso.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Límites:** Antecedente metodológico externo: no prueba una solución previa sobre BattLeDIM.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Variantes comparadas que producen presión futura:** hDA/hDS-RNN; SARIMA; SVR; GBRT; MLP/ANN; Seq2Seq; Transformer; DA/DS-RNN; DA/DS-RNN-II; DSTP-RNN.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Evidencia incompleta: no descartar ni sumar como confirmado\\r\\n\\r\\n### VAR multivariado de presión · temporal descrito\\r\\n\\r\\n**Técnica:** Vector autoregression sobre presión histórica; también menciona series exógenas.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Documentado:** Estima el patrón normal de presión para residuos/SPC.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Pendiente:** Orden/ventana exacta, lead y disponibilidad temporal de exógenas no definidos; no código alineado conservado.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidencia:** P08a, PDF pp.2–3. Incluido como temporal descrito, separado de confirmados.\\r\\n\\r\\n### AGN · estado futuro, target por aclarar\\r\\n\\r\\n**Técnica:** GGNN/GRU con atención self/external y rasgos nodales.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Documentado:** El texto editorial describe presión histórica, secuencias futuras y entrenamiento cronológico en L-Town modificada.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Pendiente:** La salida h y la equivalencia presión/head/demanda no se resuelven con el texto parcial; falta PDF/código validado.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidencia:** Consulta editorial parcial ampliada; no se acredita presión futura como confirmado.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Casos que no acreditan presión futura\\r\\n\\r\\n- **D-UKF-AW-GSI:** La medición zh es constante durante el run; k es iteración de estimación, no tiempo futuro. P18 pp.4 y 7.\\r\\n- **LILA / Leakbusters:** Presión/caudal de la condición actual → referencia actual; no shift de target.\\r\\n- **GATRes:** Mask de x y comparación contra y del mismo snapshot; no desplazamiento temporal.\\r\\n- **Artesia · ExtraTrees:** Agregados de todo el día y cross-validation por días; no origen de pronóstico prospectivo documentado.\\r\\n- **DHI China:** LSTM se utiliza para clasificar áreas con fugas, no para etiquetar presión futura.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Gaps y objetivo candidato\\r\\n\\r\\nLa predicción temporal de presión ya participa en soluciones de fugas sobre L-Town: no basta proponer “predecir presión”. Los casos confirmados usan un paso de cinco minutos; varios aprenden presión nominal saludable o escenarios modificados. Falta establecer, para nuestra comparación, el desempeño a +15 minutos sobre presión observada bajo un protocolo causal compartido, y el efecto de seleccionar sensores por relación hidráulica y antigüedad. Esa combinación es un aporte candidato, no una afirmación de novedad mundial. Se deben cerrar Tsinghua y AGN, comparar persistencia, regresión con rezagos y un antecedente adaptado a tres pasos, y medir MAE/RMSE por nodo, régimen y retraso. Un horizonte diferente por sí solo no prueba novedad.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Trazabilidad\\r\\n\\r\\nCSV: `respaldo/estado_del_arte/clasificacion/sources_classified.csv`. JSON: `classification.json`. # Registro de búsqueda y alcance

Fecha: 2026-10-11. Investigación exploratoria profunda, con seguimiento de citas y lectura selectiva de artículos/código; **no es una revisión sistemática exhaustiva ni un análisis bibliométrico de Scopus/Web of Science**.

## Estrategia aplicada

1. Leer propuesta, feedback del profesor y rúbrica.
2. Partir de publicación original, reglas, sitio del comité, Zenodo y generador/evaluador KIOS.
3. Seguir las 18 propuestas enlazadas por el comité y referencias del artículo original.
4. Buscar modelos data-driven, regresión, predicción temporal, reconstrucción, grafos, autoencoders, diccionarios, ensambles y trabajo reciente.
5. Vincular artículos a repositorios de autores; guardar commit, licencia, árbol y archivos de estudio.
6. Comprobar dataset oficial, etiquetas y unidades, y separar datasets derivados o redes diferentes.
7. Contrastar posibles aportes con antecedentes encontrados hasta 2026.

## Consultas utilizadas (selección representativa)

| Grupo | Consultas |
|---|---|
| Panorama | `BattLeDIM L-Town leak detection localization github benchmark`; `BattLeDIM L-Town machine learning review benchmark 2025 2026` |
| Participantes | `"BattLeDIM" "Leakbusters" code`; `"BattLeDIM" "github" "LILA"`; `BattLeDIM TUD algorithm github` |
| Pronóstico | `L-Town pressure prediction forecasting github BattLeDIM`; `"BattLeDIM" "pressure forecasting"`; `"L-Town" "pressure" "forecasting" learning`; `"BattLeDIM" "15-minute" forecasting` |
| Grafos | `"Graph-based learning for leak detection" github`; `"Graph-based learning for leak detection and localisation" Gardarsson`; `"Graph Neural Networks for Pressure Estimation" github` |
| Representaciones | `"Data-driven Leak Localization" "Dictionary" github Irofti`; `"BattLeDIM" "github" "autoencoder"`; `"The performance of encoder decoder neural networks" pdf` |
| Extensiones | `"BattLeDIM" "conformal prediction"`; `"L-Town" "pressure forecasting" "conformal"`; `"BattLeDIM" "forecasting" "uncertainty"`; `"BattLeDIM" "pressure prediction" random forest` |
| Búsqueda específica | `site:github.com "BattLeDIM" "RandomForestRegressor"`; `site:github.com "L-Town" "Ridge"`; `"SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning" pdf` |
| Actualización | `"Algorithm-informed graph neural networks" github`; `"Benchmarking Diverse Learning Architectures for Leak Detection and Localization"`; `"Centrality-Aware Machine Learning" github` |

Se revisaron páginas primarias del comité, Zenodo, repositorios de autores, CEUR, UCL, TU Delft, NSF, NTNU/B-WaterSmart, IFIP, MDPI y copias institucionales/de autor. Las consultas también devolvieron sitios secundarios: se utilizaron para localizar candidatos, no para reemplazar la evidencia técnica primaria.

## Inclusión y prioridad

- **Central:** trabajo sobre BattLeDIM/L-Town, tarea clara y descripción verificable; alta prioridad si hay artículo completo y código del autor.
- **Complementario:** usa topología de L-Town en nuevas simulaciones, solo Área A o reconstrucción espacial. Registrar esa diferencia.
- **Auxiliar:** otro dataset pero técnica pertinente para forecast, sensores o evaluación.
- **Candidato:** solo resumen o receta/code/datos incompletos; no presentar como reproducido.
- **Descartado como antecedente directo:** otro dataset/red o repositorio que documenta una intención sin experimentos consolidados.

## Hallazgos de búsqueda y control de calidad

1. El documento WeLL usa Hanoi, Anytown y Neptun. Se conserva como auxiliar y se excluye de resultados de L-Town.
2. Centrality-Aware ML usa Modena y otras redes (Fossolo/Net3, entre otras); no es evidencia de un pronóstico sobre las series BattLeDIM originales.
3. El benchmark amplio de arquitecturas de Ranganathan et al. (2026), DOI `10.1016/j.jwpe.2026.110114`, usa Net3. Puede orientar comparaciones, pero no se considera un resultado sobre L-Town.
4. El trabajo de ensamble interpretable 2026 usa topología L-Town en escenarios propios nocturnos; su dataset específico no está abierto según el artículo. No comparar su accuracy 0,9667 con TPR de los eventos oficiales 2019.
5. La copia de fugas 2019 de Kaggle no coincide con el archivo oficial; no se deriva una conclusión sobre su causa.
6. Artículo y README Liquor-HGNN publican métricas diferentes. Se guardan las dos versiones sin fusionar cifras.
7. Una referencia secundaria a Zenodo `11349367` devolvió por la API el registro `11349368`, de otra colección, con `Image_Pcd_Fusion.zip`. Se conserva el JSON como evidencia de enlace incorrecto y **se excluye del dataset y la bibliografía BattLeDIM**. No usarlo como supuesta versión nueva.

## Qué sigue sin demostrarse

- Que la búsqueda haya encontrado toda publicación sobre forecast de presión o sensor budgets.
- Que la extensión propuesta sea una primera contribución científica mundial.
- Que cada paper tenga un entorno o dataset completo para reproducir exactamente sus métricas.
- Que un mejor score de detección implique mejor forecast.
- Que los artículos más nuevos de acceso parcial compartan todos sus datos o código.

Las búsquedas sin coincidencia exacta no prueban ausencia de trabajos. Repetir consultas del aporte definitivo, revisar citas hacia adelante y completar acceso a los títulos más próximos antes de formular una afirmación fuerte de novedad.

El documento WeLL usa Hanoi, Anytown y Neptun. Se conserva como auxiliar y se excluye de resultados de L-Town.\\r\\n2. Centrality-Aware ML usa Modena y otras redes (Fossolo/Net3, entre otras); no es evidencia de un pronóstico sobre las series BattLeDIM originales.\\r\\n3. El benchmark amplio de arquitecturas de Ranganathan et al. (2026), DOI `10.1016/j.jwpe.2026.110114`, usa Net3. Puede orientar comparaciones, pero no se considera un resultado sobre L-Town.\\r\\n4. El trabajo de ensamble interpretable 2026 usa topología L-Town en escenarios propios nocturnos; su dataset específico no está abierto según el artículo. No comparar su accuracy 0,9667 con TPR de los eventos oficiales 2019.\\r\\n5. La copia de fugas 2019 de Kaggle no coincide con el archivo oficial; no se deriva una conclusión sobre su causa.\\r\\n6. Artículo y README Liquor-HGNN publican métricas diferentes. Se guardan las dos versiones sin fusionar cifras.\\r\\n7. Results explanation\n\n#### 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This information provides a crucial basis for subsequent analysis.\n\nImage: Fig 5\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (827KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 5. Analysis of the number of the recurrent layers: (a) shortest path from sensor nodes to non-sensor nodes; (b) MAE distribution corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers; (c) MAPE corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers.\n\nExperimental results (Figs. 5(b) and 5(c)) indicate that the best performance is not achieved at the maximum iteration of 11, but at 3 iterations of recurrent layers. This finding demonstrates that more iterations are not necessarily better; instead, they need to match the majority of the shortest path between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. When the number of iterations exceeds a certain threshold (such as 1 in this case), further increasing the number of recurrent layers has limited improvement on the model’s prediction performance. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.\n\nFurther analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the model’s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.\n\nIn summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the model’s prediction accuracy. It also effectively reduces the iteration of recurrent layers, greatly decreasing training time of the model. This advantage is crucial for predictive modeling of actual WDSs, especially for medium- and large-scale systems, where its efficiency will provide significant application value.\n\n#### 4.2.2. Hidden semantic analysis of gating mechanisms\n\nThrough an in-depth analysis of the weight matrices obtained from the trained reset gate (Eq. (6)) and the update gate (Eq. (7)), this study reveals the unique mechanism by which the model selectively retains short- and long-term information between nodes. Analysis of the model parameters reveals that the weights of the two critical gates consistently remain close to 0.5. The balance between the reset gate and update gate indicates that short- and long-term memory are equally important in the forecasting model. This finding inspires a new perspective on model simplification: replacing complex gating mechanisms with several constants. This operation will significantly reduce the model’s parameters, thereby enhancing its performance and facilitating its application in larger-scale WDSs.\n\nThe CHPS (Eq. (8)) demonstrates how the AGN model efficiently retains key information. For visualization purposes, only edges with weights greater than 0.01 are displayed in the figure, and the weights of nodes 215 were divided by 10. Attention weight of the CHPS.\n\nThe study found significant differences in the contributions of various sensor nodes during the prediction process, reflecting their interdependence and the strategic significance of sensor layout. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\n\nBy introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\n\n## 5. Conclusions\n\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\n\nIn a sustainable and cost-effective WDS, this research offers a valuable tool for efficient demand modeling for numerous cities lacking hydraulic models. Our research findings have practical implications beyond the benchmark we considered: cities seeking to optimize water distribution and enhance the management level of WDSs will benefit from the insights provided by our framework. The findings hold practical significance extending beyond the specific case studies presented: Cities aiming to optimize water distribution and improve management practices will benefit from the insights offered by our framework. Water utilities can leverage the interpretable predictions to enhance confidence in their scheduling decisions.\n\nHowever, this study still has certain limitations and gaps in practical applications: (1) potential information leakage during the model tuning process when determining optimal parameters based on model performance; (2) uncertainties related to sensor acquisition errors and infrastructure conditions; and (3) the inability to satisfy all three conservation equations at the same time.\n\nOur open-source model lays a valuable foundation for developing machine learning-based models as an alternative to hydraulic models, which future research can further optimize. Future research should focus on enhancing the architecture of GNN and applying more interpretability methods to improve both performance and clarity. Model Performance: First, the model should be tested on more real-world WDSs to verify its robustness and explore the relationship between network characteristics and optimal parameter settings. Additionally, more water network features should be extracted, and various types of sensor data should be integrated to achieve multi-source information integration, enhancing the model’s alignment with practical problems. Furthermore, incorporating physical laws can constrain the predictions, improving both the accuracy and credibility of the model. Model Interpretability: The transparency of the model directly affects its deplorability in the water distribution field, which is characterized by robustness and safety. This study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the model’s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. === PDF PAGE 1 ===
A multistage approach to detect and isolate multiple leakages in
district metering areas in water distribution systems

Xiaoting Wang1, Junyu Li2, Xipeng Yu3, Ziqing Ma4, Yujun Huang5
1,2,3,4,5 Smart Water Research Center, School of Environment, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China
1 wang-xt15@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn;

Keywords: Data-driven approach, flow and pressure estimation, leakage detection, leakage
localization, time series decomposition

ABSTRACT
Hydraulic accidents or abnormal situations, also known as leakages, cause not only water losses,
but also service interruptions and other negative effects [1]. In order to facilitate the rapid response
of water utilities and to reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely detection
method is required. Built on data-driven method and hydraulic modelling, this study proposes a
multistage approach to solve the leakage detection and localization of the battle problem in L-town.
The proposed approach is shown in fig.1, which consists of three stages:
i)
estimation of flow and pressure;
ii)
identification of abrupt and incipient leakages; and
iii)
isolation of pipes with leakages.

Figure 1. Flow chart of the multistage approach
1. Estimation of flow and pressure
As described in the L-town problem statement, the water utility has set up pressure and flow
sensors at a few predefined locations in the network. Time series data collected from these sensors
are provided. The variations in the pressure/flow data are caused by daily, weekly and seasonal water
usage patterns, leakages and other uncertain factors. To facilitate the identification of leak-induced
data from the time series, flow and pressure caused by the users, which are called flow and pressures
under normal condition, are estimated by data-driven time series methods.

=== PDF PAGE 2 ===
1.1 Flow
According to the layout of the L-town water distribution network and the locations of the sensors,
the network is divided into two relatively independent areas. Area C has sensors for total inflow, and
most nodes in the area have water meters (AMRs) at the same time, which is regarded as one zone.
Area A and B are treated as one zone, which are called area AB, because there is no separate sensor
measuring the inflow to area B. We calculate the flow in area C and area AB separately.
For area C, the difference between the total inflow and total water consumption can reflect the
leak information. The total inflow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of the
pump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the level data of the tank has not changed
before and after some time point, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to
suggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts with the data from the AMRs which show evident
water consumption in that period. This implies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize
its adverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the total inflow and total demand in
area C are calculated at intervals of 30 minutes.
The total inflow to the AB area, is recorded by three flow sensors . The actual water consumption
of users in the AB area needs to be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of
junction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as a basis for the prediction of total demand
in area AB. Specifically, the changing trend of users' water usage mode in area C during the year
should be similar to that in area AB. Therefore, the annual trend in the data in area C can be
transplanted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The water consumption of area AB can
be predicted by combining with the simulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main problem
here is to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C.
We assume that the demand in area C is composed of trend items (changes within the year),
periodic items (changes within the week), and random items (by stochastic factors), that is
(t)

where Y(t) represents the total AMR data in the C area, S(t) represents the trend term, T(t) represents
the periodic term, and R(t) represents the random term in the data.
To extract the time series items, empirical mode decomposition (EMD) has been employed in
this study. EMD can identify the periodic component of time-series data and the trend items of data,
it can decompose the original signal via subtracting the intrinsic modal function (IMF) successively,
which is the mean of the upper and lower envelopes of the signal to be decomposed [2]. The trend
items in demand mode can be represented by one of the IMFs. The trend item in the AMR data is
stripped through the multiplication model (Equation 1). Hence, the AMR data needs to be transformed.
The logarithmic transformation is used before EMD in this study. Then the trend item is extracted
from AMR data of region C. This trend item represents the annual change in water consumption in
area C (or area AB). In summary, the estimated value of water consumption in the AB area can be
calculated by the following method

where qi(t) represents the demand at node i, which is obtained through the hydraulic model. NAB

Note that areas with industrial users do not follow the same consumption pattern of the others,
hence the AMR data does not include the records of the industrial junctions, which consume huge
amounts of water and have high uncertainty. These characteristics will affect the similarity of the two
regional (area AB and area C) trend items.
1.2 Pressure
The time series of pressure sensors are influenced by both normal water usages and water
leakages. In order to select a suitable forecasting model for normal water consumption, the monitoring
data is comprehensively analyzed first [3]. The input data, which is a high dimensional time series
data, exhibits strong spatial and temporal correlation [4]. Vector autoregressive model (VAR) has
proven to be efficient in many multi-dimensional time series forecasting tasks. VAR model
regressively uses historical information and white noise to give the prediction of the current value. In

=== PDF PAGE 3 ===
the meanwhile, the effects of exogenous series are considered as well. As such, we use VAR model
to predict the normal pattern of the pressure series. In the training phase, we use a machine learning
model trained only with the normal data. Then in the test phase, the well-trained model is applied on
the whole dataset and produces the normal pattern of the pressure series. After that, we use the
residual between the observed value and the true value in the detection of abnormal value. The
residual between the estimated series and the observed series indicates the abnormal behavior of the
pressure series.
2. Identification
Flow and pressure are estimated though appropriate prediction, the estimated values are
considered as the aggregated user consumption under normal conditions. When a leakage occurs, the
abnormal flow and pressure (observed from SCADA) differ from the estimated value, as shown in
fig.2. For the flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the tank level meter are used
to calculate the total inflow of area C, and the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user
consumptions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is used to identify leakages
as residuals in area C.

Figure 2. Schematic diagram of leakage identification in Area AB. (a) Estimation of the flow; (b)
Residual between estimated and observed flow; (c) Residuals between estimated and observed
pressures.
In the pipe networks provided, the leakages are divided into two types: abrupt leakage, incipient
leakage. Corresponding to residual data, the pattern of residual series contains constant (no leak),
abrupt increase (abrupt leakage), incipient increase (incipient leakage) and abrupt decrease (repaired
leakage, the end time of leakage) . Different methods are proposed to deal with leakages of different
types. Statistical Process Control (SPC) methods are proposed to solve this leakage detection mission.
As shown in fig. 2b, although the residuals series is calculated by the difference between
observed and estimated series, it is still a time series with irregular fluctuations. In order to reduce
the nonstationary of residual series, first difference and weekly difference are calculated to make the
data smooth, as shown in fig. 3. The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified through
difference transformation, three-sigma SPC limit is used to identify the sudden rise and incline of the
flow curve [5]. Therefore, the start time (abrupt increase), the end time of leakages (abrupt decrease),
and the leak size (the abrupt value of increase or decrease) are obtained.

=== PDF PAGE 4 ===

Figure 3. Weekly difference of flow residuals in area AB.
There is one more thing to figure out, how does these start time match the end time. The equality
of the variation of the flow at the start and end of the leakage is used to match the start time and the
end time of a leakage. A complementing method to address this problem is to sort the pressure nodes
residuals, as shown in fig. 4. A sudden increase in pressure will lead to an increase in the residual
between the current value and its moving average. The variation of the pressure at the neighboring
nodes will be higher than more distant nodes. The birth and fix of a certain leakage will cause similar
patterns for the neighbor nodes and the distant nodes. By observing the pattern at each start time and
end time, a matching could be done.

Figure 4. Variation of the pressure for the matching of the start time and end time.
For incipient leakages, a window-size based SPC slope method is employed to identify the
incipient increase in flow residuals. If without leakages, the curve of residuals is likely to be smooth
and constant. The pattern of flow residuals consists of many sub-patterns caused by different leakages.
After the identification of abrupt leakages, the pattern caused by abrupt leakages could be removed
from the curve of residuals. The rest of the curve only contains the sub-pattern caused by the incipient
leakages. Their slopes could be used to distinguish different unfixed incipient leakages. Each incipient
leakage has an identical and constant slope. In this way, the start time of each incipient leakage could
be identified once the slope of the curve changes. We use a slide window of length T to go through
the curve. As it moves along the time axis, it calculates the slope in the window. A threshold is set
for the identification of the incipient leakage. As shown in Table 1, all the unfixed incipient leakage
could be identified with the above mentioned method. However, the peak of each unfixed incipient
leakages is hard to be determined because of the overlap of different incipient leakage. Therefore, an
approximate peak time is given for each incipient leakage.
Table 1. Identified slopes of the incipient leakages in area AB

3. Localization
Due to the fact that the boundaries of the three regions of the L-Town network are obvious, it is
easy to build hydraulic models of area C as it has a large number of AMRs, so we split the L-Town

=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
network into two parts, area C network and area AB network, to locate leakages separately. We first
delete the pump connecting area AB and area C, so that the original L-Town network is divided. For
area AB network, we add a node at the position of the pump with a demand pattern based on pump
flow SCADA data, and we replace the tank with a reservoir which is assigned a head pattern using
the observed tank level data in area C. Finally, we build two separated networks (areas AB and C)
to locate leakages with the same hydraulic calculation results as the L-Town network.
The hydraulic model of L-town built on EPANET tool is employed to simulate nodal pressure.
The pressure sensors' data without leakages in 2018 is used to calibrate two networks by adjusting
roughness of pipes to minimize the mean squared error (MSE) of observed and simulated pressure
values.
For area AB, it is difficult to build an accurate hydraulic model as the network has no AMRs, so
we use the idea of control variates to localize each leakage in this area. Firstly, we find two weeks
when the leakage had occurred and the leakage had not occurred based on the start and end time of
the leakage which is calculated before. Secondly, making a difference between the observed pressure
data at the corresponding time in the two weeks. We assume that the leakage to be localized is the
only leakage between the two weeks, so the difference could reflect the effect of this leakage on
pressure sensors in the network. Thirdly, two new area AB networks are created to indicate the two
weeks mentioned above by adjusting demands with season trends and adding pump flow based on
SCADA data, and some known unfixed leakages are added to the network as well. Then grid
searching is conducted for nodes and emitter coefficient. Nodes are searched one by one in area AB
network except for reservoir nodes. Emitter coefficients which are used to simulate leakages are
searched at appropriate intervals (based on the size of the leakage flow, i.e. bigger leakage flow has
bigger search intervals). A difference between the simulated pressure data is calculated in the same
way as before for the observed data. Finally, MSE of two differences, the observed values’ difference
and the simulated values’ difference, is used as evaluation function for this problem, and we could
find high probability area of leakage with low MSE value.
MSE =
∑
(∑
(∆𝑃𝑜𝑖,𝑗−∆𝑃𝑠𝑖,𝑗)2
𝑇
𝑗=1
)

(3)
Where 𝑁𝑠 is the total number of pressure sensors in area AB, 𝑇 is the total number of
simulation time steps, ∆𝑃𝑜𝑖,𝑗 is the difference of two weeks’ observed value at pressure sensor 𝑖 at
time 𝑗 and ∆𝑃𝑠𝑖,𝑗 is the difference of two weeks’ simulated value at pressure sensor 𝑖 at time 𝑗.
In area C, almost every node has an AMR, which facilitates accurate building of an hydraulic
model in EPANET. We select one week when the bust had been occurred and compare the observed
and the simulated values to determine the location of the leakage. Secondly, we assign the AMR
demand data to nodes and calculate the reservoir head pattern according to the SCADA data of tank
level. Thirdly, search for nodes and emitter coefficient and find the difference between the observed
pressure value and the simulated value. Finally, we use the same way to calculate MSE of the
difference and find high probable area of leakage.
Fig.5 shows the result of the leakage occurred at 20th April 2019, 12:00. The shade in grey in
the figure represents the size of the MSE. The node with darker color has smaller MSE. Among
them, red, orange, yellow, green and blue indicate the five nodes with the smallest MSE, that is, the
area with the highest probability of leakage.

=== PDF PAGE 6 ===

Figure 5. Schematic diagram of leakage isolation.
4. Conclusion
This study presents a method for leakage detection and isolation for the battle of L-town DMA.
Firstly, data-driven estimations of flow and pressure are proposed to extract leak-induced data from
sensor data. Empirical mode decomposition is performed to obtain the time series trend item and
estimate flow under normal conditions. Vector auto regressive is employed to extract the spatial
correlation of pressure sensors and estimate the time series data of pressure. Secondly, first difference
and weekly difference are calculated to reduce the nonstationary of residual series. For abrupt
leakages, the duration and size of leakages are identified by 3-sigma SPC method, and the start time
and the end time are matched by analysing the leak size and pressure pattern. For the detection of
incipient leakages, which usually yield gentle slope and small quantity of water loss, the window-size
slope variation method is used in this study. Thirdly, the emitter coefficient of node in EPANET is
set to simulate pipe leakage. The idea of control variates is used to find the two weeks when leakage
had occurred and not occurred respectively. The difference between the pressure data of two weeks
is calculated to represent the impact of the leakage on the pressure of the network. By the above
method, 16 leakages are detected and localized in the battle DMA networks in 2019.

=== PDF PAGE 7 ===

A multistage approach to detect and isolate multiple leakages in
district metering areas in water distribution systems

Xiaoting Wang1*, Junyu Li2, Xipeng Yu3, Ziqing Ma4, Yujun Huang5
1,2,3,4,5 Smart Water Research Center, School of Environment, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China
1 wang-xt15@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn;

SUMMARY
Hydraulic accidents or abnormal situations, also known as leakages, cause not only water losses,
but also service interruptions and other negative effects. In order to facilitate the rapid response of
water utilities and reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely detection and
isolation method is required. To solve the battle problem in L-town, this study investigates the
potential of the combination use of data-driven method and hydraulic modelling and proposes a
multistage approach, which comprises three stages: estimation, identification and localization. Firstly,
empirical mode decomposition and vector auto regressive are performed to identify the trend in flow
time series and the spatial correlation of pressure values at different sensors. The two data-driven
estimations of flow and pressure are used to extract leak-induced values from the monitoring data.
Secondly, first difference and weekly difference are calculated to reduce the nonstationary of residual
series. The duration and size of leakages are identified by analysing residuals though three-sigma
SPC method. Meanwhile, the window-size slope variation method is proposed to detect the start time
and leak size of incipient leakages. Thirdly, emitters are used to represent and simulate pipe leakages
in EPANET2. Localization algorithm, which uses the idea of control variates to find two weeks when
the leakage had occurred and not occurred, is employed to represent the impact of the leakage on the
pressure of the network by calculating the difference between the pressure data of two weeks. By
identifying the gaps between the differences of the observed values and of the simulated values, areas
with a high probability of leakage occurring are found. The proposed method is applied to L-town
network. Results show that 16 leakages are detected and localized in 2019, which demonstrates the
effectiveness of the solution for leak detection and localization in water distribution systems.

Leveraging the inherent graph structure of WDNs, recent approaches have used graph-based data-driven methods. However, these methods often learn shortcuts that work well with in-distribution data but fail to generalize to out-of-distribution data.\nL17: To address this limitation and inspired by the perfect generalization ability of classical algorithms, we propose an algorithm-informed graph neural network (AIGNN). Recognizing that WDNs function as flow networks, incorporating max-flow information can be beneficial for inferring pressures. In the proposed framework, we first train AIGNN to emulate the Ford-Fulkerson algorithm for solving max-flow problems. This algorithmic knowledge is then transferred to address the pressure estimation problem in WDNs.\nL18: Two AIGNNs are deployed, one to reconstruct pressure based on the current measurements, and another to predict pressure based on previous measurements. Leakages are detected and localized by comparing the outputs of the reconstructor and the predictor. By pretraining AIGNNs to reason like algorithms, they are expected to extract more task-relevant and generalizable features.\nL19: Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed algorithm-informed approach achieves superior results with better generalization ability compared to GNNs that do not incorporate algorithmic knowledge.\nL20: Subjects:  | Machine Learning (cs.LG)\nL21: Cite as:  | cite9†arXiv:2408.02797 [cs.LG]\nL22:    | (or cite10†arXiv:2408.02797v1 [cs.LG] for this version)\nL23:    | cite11†https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2408.02797†doi.org arXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\nL24: ## Submission history\nL25: \nL26: From: Zepeng Zhang [cite12†view email ]\nL27: [v1] Mon, 5 Aug 2024 19:25:05 UTC (996 KB)\nL28: \nL29: Full-text links:\nL30: \nL31: ## Access Paper:\nL32: \nL33: View a PDF of the paper titled Algorithm-Informed Graph Neural Networks for Leakage Detection and Localization in Water Distribution Networks, by Zepeng Zhang and 1 other authors\nL34: \nL35:   * cite7†View PDF L36:   * cite8†HTML (experimental) L37:   * cite13†TeX Source L38: \nL39: cite14†view license L40: ### Current browse context:\nL41: \nL42: cs.LG\nL43: \nL44: cite15†< prev |   cite16†next > L45: \nL46: cite17†new | cite18†recent | cite19†2024-08 L47: \nL48: Change to browse by:\nL49: \nL50: cite20†cs L51: \nL52: ### References & Citations\nL53: \nL54:   * cite21†NASA ADS†ui.adsabs.harvard.edu L55:   * cite22†Google Scholar†scholar.google.com L56:   * cite23†Semantic Scholar†api.semanticscholar.org L57: \nL58: [Button: export BibTeX citation] Loading...\nL59: \nL60: ## BibTeX formatted citation\nL61: \nL62: [Button: ×]\nL63: \nL64: loading...\nL65: \nL66: Data provided by:\nL67: \nL68: ### Bookmark\nL69: \nL70: [Input] Bibliographic Tools\nL71: # inspección estática, no ejecución', 'scope_description': 'selected source/documentation snapshot; not a full clone; no execution'}\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 1 ===\\r\\nA multistage approach to detect and isolate multiple leakages in\\r\\ndistrict metering areas in water distribution systems\\r\\n\\r\\nXiaoting Wang1, Junyu Li2, Xipeng Yu3, Ziqing Ma4, Yujun Huang5\\r\\n1,2,3,4,5 Smart Water Research Center, School of Environment, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China\\r\\n1 wang-xt15@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn;\\r\\n\\r\\nKeywords: Data-driven approach, flow and pressure estimation, leakage detection, leakage\\r\\nlocalization, time series decomposition\\r\\n\\r\\nABSTRACT\\r\\nHydraulic accidents or abnormal situations, also known as leakages, cause not only water losses,\\r\\nbut also service interruptions and other negative effects [1]. In order to facilitate the rapid response\\r\\nof water utilities and to reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely detection\\r\\nmethod is required. Built on data-driven method and hydraulic modelling, this study proposes a\\r\\nmultistage approach to solve the leakage detection and localization of the battle problem in L-town.\\r\\nThe proposed approach is shown in fig.1, which consists of three stages:\\r\\ni)\\r\\nestimation of flow and pressure;\\r\\nii)\\r\\nidentification of abrupt and incipient leakages; and\\r\\niii)\\r\\nisolation of pipes with leakages.\\r\\n\\r\\nFigure 1. Flow chart of the multistage approach\\r\\n1. Estimation of flow and pressure\\r\\nAs described in the L-town problem statement, the water utility has set up pressure and flow\\r\\nsensors at a few predefined locations in the network. Time series data collected from these sensors\\r\\nare provided. The variations in the pressure/flow data are caused by daily, weekly and seasonal water\\r\\nusage patterns, leakages and other uncertain factors. To facilitate the identification of leak-induced\\r\\ndata from the time series, flow and pressure caused by the users, which are called flow and pressures\\r\\nunder normal condition, are estimated by data-driven time series methods.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 2 ===\\r\\n1.1 Flow\\r\\nAccording to the layout of the L-town water distribution network and the locations of the sensors,\\r\\nthe network is divided into two relatively independent areas. Area C has sensors for total inflow, and\\r\\nmost nodes in the area have water meters (AMRs) at the same time, which is regarded as one zone.\\r\\nArea A and B are treated as one zone, which are called area AB, because there is no separate sensor\\r\\nmeasuring the inflow to area B. We calculate the flow in area C and area AB separately.\\r\\nFor area C, the difference between the total inflow and total water consumption can reflect the\\r\\nleak information. The total inflow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of the\\r\\npump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the level data of the tank has not changed\\r\\nbefore and after some time point, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to\\r\\nsuggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts with the data from the AMRs which show evident\\r\\nwater consumption in that period. This implies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize\\r\\nits adverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the total inflow and total demand in\\r\\narea C are calculated at intervals of 30 minutes.\\r\\nThe total inflow to the AB area, is recorded by three flow sensors . The actual water consumption\\r\\nof users in the AB area needs to be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of\\r\\njunction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as a basis for the prediction of total demand\\r\\nin area AB. Specifically, the changing trend of users' water usage mode in area C during the year\\r\\nshould be similar to that in area AB. Therefore, the annual trend in the data in area C can be\\r\\ntransplanted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The water consumption of area AB can\\r\\nbe predicted by combining with the simulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main problem\\r\\nhere is to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C.\\r\\nWe assume that the demand in area C is composed of trend items (changes within the year),\\r\\nperiodic items (changes within the week), and random items (by stochastic factors), that is\\r\\n(t)\\r\\n(t)\\r\\n(t)\\r\\n(t)\\r\\nY\\r\\nS\\r\\nT\\r\\nR\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n(1)\\r\\nwhere Y(t) represents the total AMR data in the C area, S(t) represents the trend term, T(t) represents\\r\\nthe periodic term, and R(t) represents the random term in the data.\\r\\nTo extract the time series items, empirical mode decomposition (EMD) has been employed in\\r\\nthis study. EMD can identify the periodic component of time-series data and the trend items of data,\\r\\nit can decompose the original signal via subtracting the intrinsic modal function (IMF) successively,\\r\\nwhich is the mean of the upper and lower envelopes of the signal to be decomposed [2]. The trend\\r\\nitems in demand mode can be represented by one of the IMFs. The trend item in the AMR data is\\r\\nstripped through the multiplication model (Equation 1). Hence, the AMR data needs to be transformed.\\r\\nThe logarithmic transformation is used before EMD in this study. Then the trend item is extracted\\r\\nfrom AMR data of region C. This trend item represents the annual change in water consumption in\\r\\narea C (or area AB). In summary, the estimated value of water consumption in the AB area can be\\r\\ncalculated by the following method\\r\\n,\\r\\n( )\\r\\n( )\\r\\nAB\\r\\nAB predicted\\r\\ni\\r\\ni N\\r\\nDemand\\r\\nS t\\r\\nq t\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n(2)\\r\\nwhere qi(t) represents the demand at node i, which is obtained through the hydraulic model. NAB\\r\\nrepresent the node set of region AB.\\r\\nNote that areas with industrial users do not follow the same consumption pattern of the others,\\r\\nhence the AMR data does not include the records of the industrial junctions, which consume huge\\r\\namounts of water and have high uncertainty. These characteristics will affect the similarity of the two\\r\\nregional (area AB and area C) trend items.\\r\\n1.2 Pressure\\r\\nThe time series of pressure sensors are influenced by both normal water usages and water\\r\\nleakages. In order to select a suitable forecasting model for normal water consumption, the monitoring\\r\\ndata is comprehensively analyzed first [3]. The input data, which is a high dimensional time series\\r\\ndata, exhibits strong spatial and temporal correlation [4]. Vector autoregressive model (VAR) has\\r\\nproven to be efficient in many multi-dimensional time series forecasting tasks. VAR model\\r\\nregressively uses historical information and white noise to give the prediction of the current value. In\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 3 ===\\r\\nthe meanwhile, the effects of exogenous series are considered as well. As such, we use VAR model\\r\\nto predict the normal pattern of the pressure series. In the training phase, we use a machine learning\\r\\nmodel trained only with the normal data. Then in the test phase, the well-trained model is applied on\\r\\nthe whole dataset and produces the normal pattern of the pressure series. After that, we use the\\r\\nresidual between the observed value and the true value in the detection of abnormal value. The\\r\\nresidual between the estimated series and the observed series indicates the abnormal behavior of the\\r\\npressure series.\\r\\n2. Identification\\r\\nFlow and pressure are estimated though appropriate prediction, the estimated values are\\r\\nconsidered as the aggregated user consumption under normal conditions. When a leakage occurs, the\\r\\nabnormal flow and pressure (observed from SCADA) differ from the estimated value, as shown in\\r\\nfig.2. For the flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the tank level meter are used\\r\\nto calculate the total inflow of area C, and the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user\\r\\nconsumptions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is used to identify leakages\\r\\nas residuals in area C.\\r\\n\\r\\nFigure 2. Schematic diagram of leakage identification in Area AB. (a) Estimation of the flow; (b)\\r\\nResidual between estimated and observed flow; (c) Residuals between estimated and observed\\r\\npressures.\\r\\nIn the pipe networks provided, the leakages are divided into two types: abrupt leakage, incipient\\r\\nleakage. Corresponding to residual data, the pattern of residual series contains constant (no leak),\\r\\nabrupt increase (abrupt leakage), incipient increase (incipient leakage) and abrupt decrease (repaired\\r\\nleakage, the end time of leakage) . Different methods are proposed to deal with leakages of differen…545 tokens truncated… contains the sub-pattern caused by the incipient\\r\\nleakages. Their slopes could be used to distinguish different unfixed incipient leakages. Each incipient\\r\\nleakage has an identical and constant slope. In this way, the start time of each incipient leakage could\\r\\nbe identified once the slope of the curve changes. We use a slide window of length T to go through\\r\\nthe curve. As it moves along the time axis, it calculates the slope in the window. A threshold is set\\r\\nfor the identification of the incipient leakage. As shown in Table 1, all the unfixed incipient leakage\\r\\ncould be identified with the above mentioned method. However, the peak of each unfixed incipient\\r\\nleakages is hard to be determined because of the overlap of different incipient leakage. Therefore, an\\r\\napproximate peak time is given for each incipient leakage.\\r\\nTable 1. Identified slopes of the incipient leakages in area AB\\r\\nStart time\\r\\nSlope(m^3/(h*5min))\\r\\nPeak time\\r\\nLeak type\\r\\n2018/09/10 00:00\\r\\n0.000508\\r\\n2018/11/01 21:30\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/03/28 14:15\\r\\n0.0014\\r\\n2019/04/07 14:15\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/06/14 15:10\\r\\n0.00072\\r\\n2019/10/22 15:10\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/10/25 22:20\\r\\n0.0015\\r\\n2019/12/09 22:20\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/11/09 01:10\\r\\n0.0017\\r\\n2019/11/29 01:10\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n3. Localization\\r\\nDue to the fact that the boundaries of the three regions of the L-Town network are obvious, it is\\r\\neasy to build hydraulic models of area C as it has a large number of AMRs, so we split the L-Town\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 5 ===\\r\\nnetwork into two parts, area C network and area AB network, to locate leakages separately. We first\\r\\ndelete the pump connecting area AB and area C, so that the original L-Town network is divided. For\\r\\narea AB network, we add a node at the position of the pump with a demand pattern based on pump\\r\\nflow SCADA data, and we replace the tank with a reservoir which is assigned a head pattern using\\r\\nthe observed tank level data in area C. Finally, we build two separated networks (areas AB and C)\\r\\nto locate leakages with the same hydraulic calculation results as the L-Town network.\\r\\nThe hydraulic model of L-town built on EPANET tool is employed to simulate nodal pressure.\\r\\nThe pressure sensors' data without leakages in 2018 is used to calibrate two networks by adjusting\\r\\nroughness of pipes to minimize the mean squared error (MSE) of observed and simulated pressure\\r\\nvalues.\\r\\nFor area AB, it is difficult to build an accurate hydraulic model as the network has no AMRs, so\\r\\nwe use the idea of control variates to localize each leakage in this area. Firstly, we find two weeks\\r\\nwhen the leakage had occurred and the leakage had not occurred based on the start and end time of\\r\\nthe leakage which is calculated before. Secondly, making a difference between the observed pressure\\r\\ndata at the corresponding time in the two weeks. We assume that the leakage to be localized is the\\r\\nonly leakage between the two weeks, so the difference could reflect the effect of this leakage on\\r\\npressure sensors in the network. Thirdly, two new area AB networks are created to indicate the two\\r\\nweeks mentioned above by adjusting demands with season trends and adding pump flow based on\\r\\nSCADA data, and some known unfixed leakages are added to the network as well. Then grid\\r\\nsearching is conducted for nodes and emitter coefficient. Nodes are searched one by one in area AB\\r\\nnetwork except for reservoir nodes. Emitter coefficients which are used to simulate leakages are\\r\\nsearched at appropriate intervals (based on the size of the leakage flow, i.e. bigger leakage flow has\\r\\nbigger search intervals). A difference between the simulated pressure data is calculated in the same\\r\\nway as before for the observed data. Finally, MSE of two differences, the observed values’ difference\\r\\nand the simulated values’ difference, is used as evaluation function for this problem, and we could\\r\\nfind high probability area of leakage with low MSE value.\\r\\nMSE =\\r\\n∑\\r\\n(∑\\r\\n(∆𝑃𝑜𝑖,𝑗−∆𝑃𝑠𝑖,𝑗)2\\r\\n𝑇\\r\\n𝑗=1\\r\\n)\\r\\n𝑁𝑠\\r\\n𝑖=1\\r\\n𝑁𝑠∙𝑇\\r\\n(3)\\r\\nWhere 𝑁𝑠 is the total number of pressure sensors in area AB, 𝑇 is the total number of\\r\\nsimulation time steps, ∆𝑃𝑜𝑖,𝑗 is the difference of two weeks’ observed value at pressure sensor 𝑖 at\\r\\ntime 𝑗 and ∆𝑃𝑠𝑖,𝑗 is the difference of two weeks’ simulated value at pressure sensor 𝑖 at time 𝑗.\\r\\nIn area C, almost every node has an AMR, which facilitates accurate building of an hydraulic\\r\\nmodel in EPANET. We select one week when the bust had been occurred and compare the observed\\r\\nand the simulated values to determine the location of the leakage. Secondly, we assign the AMR\\r\\ndemand data to nodes and calculate the reservoir head pattern according to the SCADA data of tank\\r\\nlevel. Thirdly, search for nodes and emitter coefficient and find the difference between the observed\\r\\npressure value and the simulated value. Finally, we use the same way to calculate MSE of the\\r\\ndifference and find high probable area of leakage.\\r\\nFig.5 shows the result of the leakage occurred at 20th April 2019, 12:00. The shade in grey in\\r\\nthe figure represents the size of the MSE. The node with darker color has smaller MSE. Among\\r\\nthem, red, orange, yellow, green and blue indicate the five nodes with the smallest MSE, that is, the\\r\\narea with the highest probability of leakage.\\r\\n\\r\\n=== PDF PAGE 6 ===\\r\\n\\r\\nFigure 5. Schematic diagram of leakage isolation.\\r\\n4. Conclusion\\r\\nThis study presents a method for leakage detection and isolation for the battle of L-town DMA.\\r\\nFirstly, data-driven estimations of flow and pressure are proposed to extract leak-induced data from\\r\\nsensor data. Empirical mode decomposition is performed to obtain the time series trend item and\\r\\nestimate flow under normal conditions. Vector auto regressive is employed to extract the spatial\\r\\ncorrelation of pressure sensors and estimate the time series data of pressure. Secondly, first difference\\r\\nand weekly difference are calculated to reduce the nonstationary of residual series. For abrupt\\r\\nleakages, the duration and size of leakages are identified by 3-sigma SPC method, and the start time\\r\\nand the end time are matched by analysing the leak size and pressure pattern. For the detection of\\r\\nincipient leakages, which usually yield gentle slope and small quantity of water loss, the window-size\\r\\nslope variation method is used in this study. Thirdly, the emitter coefficient of node in EPANET is\\r\\nset to simulate pipe leakage. The idea of control variates is used to find the two weeks when leakage\\r\\nhad occurred and not occurred respectively. The difference between the pressure data of two weeks\\r\\nis calculated to represent the impact of the leakage on the pressure of the network. By the above\\r\\nmethod, 16 leakages are detected and localized in the battle DMA networks in 2019.\\r\\n\\r\\nREFERENCES\\r\\n[1] M. Bakker, E. A. Trietsch, J. H. G. Vreeburg, and L. C. Rietveld, \\\"Analysis of historic leakages\\r\\nand leakage detection in water supply areas of different size\\\", Water Science & Technology: Water\\r\\nSupply, vol. 14, no. 6, pp. 1035-1044, 2014.\\r\\n[2] N. E. Huang, Z. Shen, S. R. Long, M. C. Wu, H. H. Shih, Q. Zheng, N. Yen, C. C. Tung, and H.\\r\\nH. Wu, S.M. Liu, X. Wu, Y.F. Liu, and Y.S.
